description: "How one of the ZX Spectrum Next's three AY sound chips works: its sixteen registers and the ports that reach them, the counters that make its three tones, the shift register behind its noise, the mixer register that combines them, the logarithmic volume table, the envelope and its sixteen shapes, the differences between the AY-3-8910 and YM2149 modes, how programs make music with it, and how Klive emulates it."
---

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# One AY Chip: Tones, Noise and Envelopes

Sixteen registers. Write three numbers, and the chip plays a chord forever, with no further help from the
CPU. The whole soundtrack of the ZX Spectrum 128K was made from three counters, a random-number generator and
a staircase. So how can so little make music?

The AY chip is the sound of the 128K era and of most Spectrum music since: game soundtracks, demo music, the
trackers musicians still write in today. It is a **generator** (the overview's word): once your program has
set it up, it plays by itself, and the CPU is free to run the game. That is the whole point. A beeper tune
keeps the CPU busy for every single edge of the wave; an AY tune costs a few register writes a frame, and the
game hardly notices that the band is playing. The Next has three of these chips. Here we take one of them
apart; the next chapter,
[TurboSound](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/turbosound/), puts the three together.

Read [The Big Picture: Four Sources, One Mixer](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/overview/) first: it introduces the
four kinds of source and the difference between a generator and a level writer. If square waves and DACs are
new to you, the background chapter
[How a Computer Makes Sound](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/) explains them. And where
the chip's output goes next is the subject of
[The Mixer: One Sum, Three Outputs](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/).

## The road ahead

We start with the parts of one chip and the sixteen registers a program reaches them through, by way of the
128K's two ports. Then we follow the sound from its sources: the clock that drives everything, a counter that
turns a period into a square wave, and a shift register that makes noise. The mixer register decides what
each channel plays, with one case that surprises almost everyone, and the volume table turns a volume into a
level on a curve rather than a straight line. Then comes the envelope, a volume that moves by itself in
sixteen shapes; if you read only one section, make it that one. After a look at the two chips the Next can
pretend to be, the AY-3-8910 and the YM2149, we make some music: a chord, the classic effects, and a bass line
played by the envelope itself. We end with how Klive does the same.

<Callout type="info">
The chip is in **YM2149 mode** (the state after power-on) unless a section says otherwise. Its counters step
**218,750 times a second**. Levels are in the **units of the Next's mixer**: one channel at full volume adds
255. The waveforms are captured from Klive's emulated Next at 48,000 samples a second, from programs checked
to do what the text says; they show Klive's mix, where the AY chips count up from 0 as they do on the
hardware.
</Callout>

## The parts of one chip

The chip was designed by General Instrument as the **AY-3-8910**, a programmable sound generator, or PSG;
Yamaha made a compatible chip, the **YM2149**. The 128K used the first. The Next builds both into its FPGA,
and a register chooses which one each of its three chips behaves as (you meet it in AY or YM, further on).

Inside, one chip has a handful of parts, all driven by one clock:

<figure>
{/* fig:block */}

On the left, the clock of 218,750 ticks a second feeds three tone generators, one per channel, the noise generator and the envelope generator. The tone generators and the noise generator go into the mixer register R7, which makes one on/off signal per channel. The envelope's level goes to all three channels' amplitude registers, each of which picks a fixed volume or the envelope's; the volume table turns that into a level; the three outputs A, B and C, 0 to 255 each, leave the chip on the right." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="434" y="310" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">the envelope's level,</text>
  <text x="434" y="326.5" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">shared by A, B and C</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:block */}
<figcaption>One AY chip. Three tone generators and one noise generator make on/off signals; the mixer register picks tone, noise or both for each channel; each channel's volume is fixed or follows the envelope; a table turns the volume into a level; three outputs, A, B and C, go to the mixer.</figcaption>
</figure>

- Three **tone generators**, one per channel, each a counter that makes a square wave at a pitch your program
  sets.
- One **noise generator**, a shift register that makes random bits, shared by the three channels.
- The **mixer register**, which decides for each channel whether it gets its tone, the noise, both, or
  neither.
- An **amplitude** per channel: a fixed volume, or the **envelope**, a volume that rises and falls by itself;
  one envelope generator is shared by the three channels.
- A **volume table**, the chip's DAC, which turns each channel's 4- or 5-bit volume into a level.
- Three **outputs**, channels **A**, **B** and **C**, each 0 to 255 units, which go to the mixer.

Here is something to notice: every one of these parts is a small counter or a register. None of them can make a
curve. Everything the chip plays is built from on/off signals and steps of volume, and that is exactly why the
AY sounds the way it does.

## Sixteen registers behind two ports

The chip has **16 registers**, R0 to R15, each 8 bits wide, though many use fewer:

<figure>
{/* fig:regmap */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 332" role="img" aria-label="The sixteen registers in two columns, each with the bits it uses drawn as boxes, bit 7 on the left. R0, R2 and R4, the low 8 bits of the tone periods of channels A, B and C; R1, R3 and R5, their top 4 bits. R6, the noise period, 5 bits. R7, the mixer, 8 bits: the I/O directions in bits 7 and 6, noise off for C, B, A in bits 5 to 3, tone off for C, B, A in bits 2 to 0. R8, R9 and R10, the amplitudes, 5 bits. R11 and R12, the envelope period's low and high bytes. R13, the envelope shape, 4 bits. R14 and R15, the I/O ports A and B, 8 bits each." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>
  <text x="0" y="46" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">R0</text>

</svg>
{/* /fig:regmap */}
<figcaption>The sixteen registers, with the bits each one uses. Orange bits make sound; grey outlined bits are the I/O ports and their directions; faint bits do nothing.</figcaption>
</figure>

Your program reaches them through two ports, the 128K's own:

1. It writes the register's number to **port `$FFFD`**, the register select port. The value must have bits
   7–5 clear: it is a register number, 0 to 31. Only 0 to 15 exist; a write to a register numbered 16 to 31
   is ignored.
2. It writes the value to **port `$BFFD`**, the data port. The selected register stays selected, so the
   program can write it again without selecting it again.
3. It reads the selected register back from port `$FFFD`. (On the +3's timing, port `$BFFD` reads the same.)

A value written to port `$FFFD` with bit 7 set is not a register number; the Next uses those values to choose
between its three chips (the next chapter's
[Three chips, two ports](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/turbosound/#three-chips-two-ports)).
Values with bits 7–5 anything else but `000` or the chip-select pattern select nothing.

After a reset, every register is 0 except **R7, which is `$FF`**: all tones and noise switched off. With every
volume at 0, the chip sits in polite silence until your program sets it up.

**R14 and R15** are not sound registers at all: they are the chip's two 8-bit **I/O ports**, A and B. On the
128K, port A drove the serial connector. On the Next, nothing is connected to them. R7 bits 6 and 7 make each
one an output (1) or an input (0); an input reads `$FF`, as if every pin were pulled up, and an output reads
back the value last written.

## The clock that drives everything

The chip runs from the Next's **1.75 MHz** PSG clock, the 28 MHz master clock divided by 16. Inside, the chip
divides it by 8 again, so its counters step **218,750 times a second**. Let's call one of those steps a
**tick** of the chip. Every pitch, every noise and every envelope in the rest of this chapter is counted in
ticks.

## A counter that makes a square wave

Each channel's **tone period** is a 12-bit number: the low 8 bits in R0 (channel A), R2 (B) or R4 (C), the top
4 bits in R1, R3 or R5. The tone generator is a counter that counts ticks:

1. At every tick, the counter goes up by one.
2. When it has counted to the period (it has counted `period` ticks since it last started), it starts again
   from 0, and the channel's square wave **flips**: high becomes low, or low becomes high.
3. So each half of the square wave lasts `period` ticks, and a whole period lasts twice that.

Here is a period of 3, tick by tick:

<figcaption>A tone generator with period 3, worked out tick by tick. The counter goes 0, 1, 2 and starts again, and the square wave flips each time it does: 3 ticks per half, 6 per period.</figcaption>
</figure>

The pitch is therefore

> frequency = 218,750 ÷ (2 × period)

Let's tune up. The A of 440 Hz needs a period of 218,750 ÷ 880 = 248.6, so 249, which plays 218,750 ÷ 498 =
**439.3 Hz**: as close as a whole number allows, and too little out of tune to hear. Captured from the machine:

<figcaption>Channel A with period 249 at full volume, captured: a square wave of 439.3 Hz, each half 249 ticks of the chip (1.14 ms), swinging from 0 to 255 units.</figcaption>
</figure>

The 12 bits give periods from 1 to 4,095, and so pitches from 218,750 ÷ 8,190 = **26.7 Hz**, a low growl, up
to 109,375 Hz, far above hearing. A period of 0 plays as 1. The steps
between pitches are fine at the bottom of the range and coarse at the top: 249 and 250 are 1.8 Hz apart, but
10 and 11 are almost 1,000 Hz apart, so very high notes come out of tune.

The 128K's AY ran from a clock of about 1.77 MHz instead of 1.75 MHz. The same period plays about 1.3 % lower
on the Next, a quarter of a semitone: a 128K tune keeps its tune, just a little lower.

## Noise from a shift register

The noise generator makes random bits with a **17-bit shift register**:

<figure>
{/* fig:lfsr */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 150" role="img" aria-label="Seventeen boxes in a row, bit 16 on the left and bit 0 on the right. Every step, each bit moves one place to the right. Bit 0 leaves the register on the right as the noise signal. Bit 0 and bit 2 are taken into an exclusive-or, whose result goes back round to become the new bit 16." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

  <text x="527" y="95" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">1</text>

  <text x="561" y="95" textAnchor="middle" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="13.5" fontWeight="bold">0</text>
  <text x="289" y="136" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="15">every step: each bit moves one place right →</text>
  <path d="M578,90 L644,90" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.8" markerEnd="url(#ay-lfsr-1)" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <text x="700" y="95" textAnchor="end" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="15" fontWeight="bold">noise</text>
  <rect x="473" y="13" width="108" height="30" rx="15" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="2" />
  <text x="527" y="33" textAnchor="middle" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="13.5">exclusive or</text>
  <path d="M561,74 L561,47" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.8" markerEnd="url(#ay-lfsr-1)" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <path d="M493,74 L493,47" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.8" markerEnd="url(#ay-lfsr-1)" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <path d="M473,28 L17,28 L17,70" fill="none" stroke="#e8912d" strokeWidth="1.8" markerEnd="url(#ay-lfsr-1)" strokeLinejoin="round" />
  <text x="34" y="20" fill="#e8912d" fontSize="15">the new bit 16</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:lfsr */}
<figcaption>The noise generator's 17-bit shift register. At every step the bits move one place towards bit 0; the new top bit is bit 0 exclusive-or bit 2; bit 0 is the noise signal.</figcaption>
</figure>

1. At every **noise step**, all 17 bits move one place towards bit 0, and the bit that falls out of bit 0 is
   lost.
2. The new bit 16 is **bit 0 exclusive-or bit 2**, taken before the move. (If the register is all zeros, as it
   is after a reset, a 1 is fed in instead, so that it gets going.)
3. **Bit 0 is the noise signal**: on or off, like a tone.

The bits it produces look random, though they are worked out exactly: the pattern repeats only after
**114,681** steps. Each step changes the output with a chance of about one half. Randomness on a budget, and
nobody can tell.

## The noise period

The noise generator's counter runs at half the tone generators' rate, 109,375 steps a second, and the **noise
period** in R6 bits 4–0 (1 to 31; 0 plays as 1) divides it once more: the register steps 109,375 ÷ period times
a second. A small period gives a high, hissing noise; a large one a slower, rougher one, closer to a rumble:

<figure>
{/* fig:noise */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 274" role="img" aria-label="Two captures of noise on channel A, ten milliseconds each, from 0 to 255 units. {/* /fig:noise */}
<figcaption>Noise on channel A, captured, 10 ms each. With period 1 the register steps 109,375 times a second and the level changes faster than the samples can follow; with period 31 it steps 3,528 times a second, and the runs of on and off are long enough to see.</figcaption>
</figure>

Even at period 31, the pattern takes 114,681 ÷ 3,528 = 32.5 seconds to repeat, so no ear ever hears it come
round. Keep in mind, though, that there is only one noise generator per chip: its period is the same for every
channel that uses it.

## Tone, noise, both or neither

**R7**, the mixer register, decides what each channel plays. Bits 0–2 switch the tones of A, B and C, bits 3–5
their noise, and both are **active low**: a 0 switches the source on. Each channel's signal is

> (tone, or tone switched off) and (noise, or noise switched off)

which gives four cases:

- **Tone only** (tone bit 0, noise bit 1): the square wave.
- **Noise only** (tone bit 1, noise bit 0): the noise.
- **Tone and noise** (both 0): on only where the tone *and* the noise are on, so a noise that comes and goes at
  the tone's pitch: a pitched hiss, used for drums and engines.
- **Neither** (both 1): the signal is always on, so the channel plays its **volume as a steady level**.

Captured on channel A, with the same tone (period 109) and noise (period 8) each time:

<figcaption>Channel A with R7 set four ways, captured, 10 ms each. Tone and noise together is the noise only while the tone is high; with neither, the channel holds its volume as a steady level.</figcaption>
</figure>

Look at the last row. Uhm, a channel with everything switched off is not silent? Exactly, and it has two
consequences that catch programmers out:

- **A channel is silenced by its volume, not by R7.** Switching off a channel's tone and noise does not make it
  silent: it makes it a steady level, which you hear as a click when it appears and disappears. The silent way
  is volume 0. Let me warn you: this catches almost everyone out once.
- **The steady level is a DAC.** A program that switches off a channel's tone and noise and then rewrites its
  volume many times a second plays a waveform through the volume table: that is how 128K programs played
  sampled drums and speech on the AY, before DAC sound cards.

R7 bits 6 and 7 are not sound: they set the direction of the I/O ports, as Sixteen registers behind two ports
explains.

## Sixteen volumes on a curve

Each channel's **amplitude register**, R8 (A), R9 (B) or R10 (C), sets its loudness:

- **Bits 3–0**: a fixed volume, 0 to 15. 0 is silent.
- **Bit 4**: when it is 1, the channel ignores bits 3–0 and follows the envelope (the next section).

The volume is not the level. It goes through the chip's **volume table**, which turns it into the level that
reaches the mixer. The table is **logarithmic**: each step up multiplies the level by roughly the same factor.
Why? Because your ear hears loudness as a ratio: doubling a level sounds like the same step up whether it goes
from 32 to 64 or from 128 to 255. On a straight scale, the top volumes would sound almost alike, and the bottom
ones would jump.

The YM2149 has a table of 32 entries, and a fixed volume `v` uses entry 2 × `v` + 1, every other entry. The
AY-3-8910 has a table of 16, one per volume. They are not the same:

<figure>
{/* fig:volume */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 354" role="img" aria-label="A chart of the level each fixed volume 0 to 15 gives, in mixer units from 0 to 255. Two lines of points: YM2149 mode, which uses every other entry of its 32-entry table, and AY-3-8910 mode, its own 16 entries. Both start at 0 and end at 255 and curve upwards, rising slowly at first and steeply at the top. The AY line lies above the YM line at every volume from 1 to 14: volume 8 gives 40 in AY mode and 27 in YM mode." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>The level each fixed volume gives, in mixer units, in YM2149 mode (red) and AY-3-8910 mode (orange). Both reach 255 at volume 15, but AY mode is louder at every volume below it.</figcaption>
</figure>

At volume 15, both give 255. Below it, the AY table is louder: volume 8 gives 40 in AY mode and 27 in YM mode,
volume 1 gives 3 against 2. So a 128K tune that uses middle volumes for its accompaniment sounds more balanced
in AY mode, the chip it was written for.

## The envelope: a volume that moves

An **envelope** is a volume that moves by itself. A channel whose amplitude register has bit 4 set takes its
volume from the chip's one **envelope generator**, which steps up or down at a rate your program sets, in one
of sixteen **shapes**.

The **envelope period** is a 16-bit number, R11 (the low byte) and R12 (the high byte). Like a tone counter,
the envelope's counter counts ticks, and every `period` ticks (0 plays as 1) the envelope takes one **step**.
A ramp from silence to full volume, or back, is **32 steps**, so it lasts

> 32 × period ÷ 218,750 seconds

from 146 µs at period 1 to 9.6 seconds at 65,535. A drum hit needs a few milliseconds; a slowly fading note a
second or more. Plenty of room either way.

## The sixteen envelope shapes

R13 bits 3–0 choose the shape, and each bit has a name:

- **ATT** (attack, bit 2): 1, the envelope starts at the bottom and climbs; 0, it starts at the top and falls.
- **CONT** (continue, bit 3): 0, after one ramp it drops to 0 and stays there, whatever the other two bits
  say.
- **HOLD** (bit 0), with CONT set: after one ramp it stops and holds its level.
- **ALT** (alternate, bit 1), with CONT set: the next ramp goes the other way. With HOLD, the envelope holds the
  level at the *opposite* end of where the ramp finished.

Let's work through two shapes:

- **Shape 13**, `1101` (CONT, ATT, HOLD): it starts at the bottom and climbs (ATT); at the top it stops (HOLD),
  and stays at full volume. The sound of a note that swells and then sustains.
- **Shape 14**, `1110` (CONT, ATT, ALT): it climbs; at the top, CONT keeps it going and ALT turns it round, so
  it falls; at the bottom it turns again. A triangle, forever.

Here are all sixteen, captured from the machine with the same period:

  <text x="0" y="534" fill="currentColor" fontSize="13.5">bits: CONT ATT ALT HOLD (R13 bits 3–0); 10 ms each, a ramp of 2.9 ms</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:envgrid */}
<figcaption>The sixteen envelope shapes, captured from the machine, 10 ms each with a period of 20 ticks (a ramp of 2.9 ms). Shapes 0 to 3 play as shape 9, and shapes 4 to 7 as shape 15, so there are eight different ones.</figcaption>
</figure>

With CONT clear, ALT and HOLD do nothing, so shapes 0–3 are all "fall once, then silence", the same as shape 9,
and shapes 4–7 are all "climb once, then silence", the same as shape 15. Did you notice that the ramps curve
instead of running straight? Every step goes through the logarithmic volume table, so the level grows by a
factor at each step, which your ear hears as an even climb. Sixteen shapes, eight sounds:

- Fall once and stay silent (0–3, 9), or climb once and fall silent (4–7, 15): a plucked or struck note.
- Fall, again and again (8), or climb, again and again (12): a saw tooth.
- Fall and climb (10), or climb and fall (14), forever: a triangle.
- Fall once and jump to full volume (11), or climb once and stay there (13).

**Writing R13 restarts the envelope**, even with the value it already holds: it goes back to the start of its
shape at once. That is how your program strikes a new note with the same shape: one write per note.

## 32 steps or 16

In YM2149 mode, the envelope's 32 steps go through the 32-entry table, each one to its own level. In AY-3-8910
mode the table has 16 entries, so the envelope uses only its top four bits: each level lasts two steps. The
ramp takes exactly as long, but it climbs in 16 coarser steps:

<figure>
{/* fig:climb */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 314" role="img" aria-label="Two captures of envelope shape 13, climb and hold, with a period of 60 ticks, 12.5 milliseconds each, from 0 to 255 units. AY-3-8910 mode: a coarser staircase of 16 steps, each twice as long, reaching 255 at the same moment." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>Shape 13 (climb and hold) with a period of 60 ticks, captured in both modes. In YM2149 mode it climbs in 32 steps of 274 µs; in AY-3-8910 mode in 16 steps of twice that. Both reach full volume at the same moment, 8.8 ms after the write.</figcaption>
</figure>

The YM's ramp shows fewer than 32 distinct levels at its bottom, because its table's lowest entries come in
equal pairs (1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3): there are 29 different levels in all.

## AY or YM

The **Peripheral 2 Setting register, NextReg `$06`**, bits 1–0, sets what all three of the Next's chips are:

- `00` (the power-on value): **YM2149**.
- `01`: **AY-3-8910**.
- `10`: the register list calls it "ZXN-8950", but the chips look only at bit 0, so it behaves exactly as
  YM2149. There is no third kind of chip, however exotic the name sounds.
- `11`: **holds all three chips in reset**: silent, registers cleared, writes ignored, until the mode is
  changed again.

What your program can see change between the two modes:

- **The volume table** (Sixteen volumes on a curve): AY mode is louder below volume 15.
- **The envelope's resolution** (32 steps or 16): 16 levels in AY mode, 32 in YM mode, over the same time.
- **The bits a register reads back.** In YM mode, every register reads back all 8 bits as written. In AY mode,
  the bits a register does not use read as 0: R1, R3, R5 and R13 keep their low 4 bits, R6, R8, R9 and R10
  their low 5 bits; the others read whole.
- **Registers 16 to 31**, which do not exist: in YM mode they read `$FF`; in AY mode they read register
  number − 16, as if the top bit of the number were ignored. Writes to them are dropped in both modes.

So how does a program find out which chip it is on? It writes a value with the top bits set to R1, say, and
reads it back: whole in YM mode, its low 4 bits in AY mode.

One more read is the Next's own: **port `$BFF5`** (an address that the data port `$BFFD` also answers) reads
the selected chip's identity in bits 7–6 and the selected register's number in bits 4–0, for software that
must know which chip and register it last selected (the next chapter's
[Three chips, two ports](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/turbosound/#three-chips-two-ports)).

## Playing a chord

Three channels, three notes. C, E and G above middle C are periods 209, 166 and 140 (523.3, 658.9 and
781.3 Hz). Set once, they play until something changes them:

<figure>
{/* fig:chord */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 398" role="img" aria-label="Four rows over six milliseconds. <figcaption>A C major chord, captured: channel A plays C (523.3 Hz), B plays E (658.9 Hz), C plays G (781.3 Hz), each alone at full volume, and the three added together, the sum a chip in mono gives both sides (the TurboSound chapter's Where the channels go).</figcaption>
</figure>

A music player does not touch the chip while a note sounds. Once a frame, 50 times a second, it moves on in the
tune: when a note ends, it writes the next note's period and volume, or restarts the envelope with R13. A few
register writes per frame is the whole cost of the music to the CPU. Not a bad deal for a band.

## Effects, one register at a time

The once-a-frame player is also how the classic effects are made, by changing a register a little every frame:

- **Vibrato**: the period moves up and down by one or two around the note, a wobble in pitch.
- **Tremolo**: the volume moves up and down, a wobble in loudness.
- **Arpeggio**: one channel cycles through the notes of a chord, a new one every frame or two, so a single
  voice sounds like a chord. Three channels can then play six or nine "notes", the chip-music sound.
- **Slides**: the period changes by a fixed amount every frame, for pitch bends and laser sounds.

## Bass from the envelope

The envelope does not have to be slow. With a short period, a repeating shape is itself a waveform. Shape 8, a
falling saw tooth, repeats every 32 steps, so with a period of 14 it plays 218,750 ÷ (32 × 14) = **488.3 Hz**.
Shape 10, a triangle, takes 64 steps for one cycle, so a period of 7 gives the same pitch:

{/* /fig:buzz */}
<figcaption>The envelope as a waveform, captured: shape 8 with a period of 14 ticks, a saw tooth, and shape 10 with a period of 7, a triangle, both at 488.3 Hz. Neither has a tone switched on: each is a steady channel whose volume follows the envelope.</figcaption>
</figure>

These waves sound very different from the square tones, and much richer, which made the "buzzer" bass a
favourite of AY musicians. The price is that there is only one envelope per chip: a channel that plays the bass
takes the envelope away from the other two. Musicians often switch on the channel's tone as well, at a pitch
related to the envelope's: the mixer register then lets the envelope's wave through only while the tone is
high, which gives yet another colour. I think it is one of the cleverest tricks ever played on this chip.

## How Klive does it

Klive's Next core is a C program compiled to WebAssembly; its three AY chips are one module, written to follow
the hardware step for step.

Each chip keeps the same state as the hardware: its sixteen registers, its three tone counters and their
outputs, the noise generator's counter and shift register, and the envelope's counter, level and direction. One
**tick** of the emulated chip is one tick of the real one, every 128 master clocks. The chip is not ticked in
step with the CPU, though: it is brought up to date when something needs it, a write to one of its ports or the
moment a sample of sound is due, by running all the ticks that have passed since the last time.

The chip's outputs change at its ticks, 218,750 times a second, far more often than a sample is taken. For the
audio jack and the speaker, Klive averages each channel's level over the time a sample covers, as the smoothing
after the real converter would; for HDMI it takes the level at the sample's moment, as HDMI does. The levels go
to the mixer as the hardware's are, from 0 upwards: unlike the beeper, Klive does not remove the AY's constant
level (the mixer chapter's
[How Klive does it](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#how-klive-does-it) explains
why). And if you want to read the code yourself, here is where it lives:

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The three chips: registers, ports, counters, noise, envelope, volume tables, AY and YM modes | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-psg.c` |
| Ports `$FFFD`, `$BFFD` and `$BFF5` | `zxnext-ports.c` in the same folder |
| Where the outputs are added | `zxnext-audio-mixer.c` in the same folder |

## What to take home

Let's take stock. One AY chip has **three tone generators**, **one noise generator**, a **mixer
register**, a fixed volume or the **envelope** for each channel, and a logarithmic **volume table**. Out come
three outputs, A, B and C, each 0 to 255 units. Your program reaches its **16 registers** through **port
`$FFFD`**, which selects one (bits 7–5 clear), and **port `$BFFD`**, which writes it; reads come from port
`$FFFD`. After a reset, every register is 0 except R7, which is `$FF`.

Everything counts in ticks, **218,750 a second** (1.75 MHz ÷ 8). A tone's 12-bit **period** plays
218,750 ÷ (2 × period), so 249 is A at 439.3 Hz, and a period of 0 plays as 1. **Noise** comes from a 17-bit
shift register fed with bit 0 exclusive-or bit 2, stepping 109,375 ÷ period times a second, with the period
in R6, 1 to 31. **R7** switches each channel's tone and noise, active low, and a channel with neither plays its
**volume as a steady level**: silence is volume 0, not R7.

R8 to R10 hold a fixed volume, 0 to 15 (YM entry 2v + 1, or the AY's own table, louder below 15), or set
bit 4 to follow the envelope. The **envelope** takes a step every period ticks (R11 and R12, 16 bits), 32 steps
a ramp, and **R13** picks one of 16 shapes from CONT, ATT, ALT and HOLD, eight of them different; every write
to R13 restarts it. **NextReg `$06` bits 1–0** make the chips YM2149 (`00` or `10`), AY-3-8910 (`01`, with a
16-level envelope and masked reads), or hold them all in reset (`11`).

A few writes per frame play three voices; vibrato, tremolo and arpeggios rewrite registers frame by frame; and
a short envelope period turns the envelope into a **saw-tooth or triangle bass**. Klive ticks each chip as the
hardware does, brings it up to date whenever a port or a sample needs it, and averages each sample's level.
Three counters, a random-number generator and a staircase, and the rest, as they say, is 54:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The volume is not the level. It goes through the chip’s ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"volume table"}],", which turns it into the level that\nreaches the mixer. The table is ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"logarithmic"}],": each step up multiplies the level by roughly the same factor.\nWhy? Because your ear hears loudness as a ratio: doubling a level sounds like the same step up whether it goes\nfrom 32 to 64 or from 128 to 255. On a straight scale, the top volumes would sound almost alike, and the bottom\nones would jump."]}]

7c:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"These waves sound very different from the square tones, and much richer, which made the “buzzer” bass a\nfavourite of AY musicians. The price is that there is only one envelope per chip: a channel that plays the bass\ntakes the envelope away from the other two. Musicians often switch on the channel’s tone as well, at a pitch\nrelated to the envelope’s: the mixer register then lets the envelope’s wave through only while the tone is\nhigh, which gives yet another colour. I think it is one of the cleverest tricks ever played on this chip."}]

7f:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Each chip keeps the same state as the hardware: its sixteen registers, its three tone counters and their\noutputs, the noise generator’s counter and shift register, and the envelope’s counter, level and direction. One\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"tick"}]," of the emulated chip is one tick of the real one, every 128 master clocks. The chip is not ticked in\nstep with the CPU, though: it is brought up to date when something needs it, a write to one of its ports or the\nmoment a sample of sound is due, by running all the ticks that have passed since the last time."]}]

84:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Everything counts in ticks, ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"218,750 a second"}]," (1.75 MHz ÷ 8). A tone’s 12-bit ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"period"}]," plays\n218,750 ÷ (2 × period), so 249 is A at 439.3 Hz, and a period of 0 plays as 1. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Noise"}]," comes from a 17-bit\nshift register fed with bit 0 exclusive-or bit 2, stepping 109,375 ÷ period times a second, with the period\nin R6, 1 to 31. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"R7"}]," switches each channel’s tone and noise, active low, and a channel with neither plays its\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"volume as a steady level"}],": silence is volume 0, not R7."]}]
85:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["R8 to R10 hold a fixed volume, 0 to 15 (YM entry 2v + 1, or the AY’s own table, louder below 15), or set\nbit 4 to follow the envelope. The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"envelope"}]," takes a step every period ticks (R11 and R12, 16 bits), 32 steps\na ramp, and ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"R13"}]," picks one of 16 shapes from CONT, ATT, ALT and HOLD, eight of them different; every write\nto R13 restarts it.
